**Answer: `confidence = successRate × min(1, attemptCount / 10)`, clamped to [0, 1].**

The code is in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:178-186`, inside `assessCompetence()`:

```ts
if (level.successRate >= cfg.competenceThreshold) {
  return safeDecision({
    useArchimedes: true,
    reason: `Archimedes success rate ... meets threshold ...`,
    confidence: clamp01(level.successRate * Math.min(1, level.attemptCount / 10)),
    ...
  });
}
```

How the pieces are built:

- **`successRate`** — `buildLevelFromEpisodes()` (competence.ts:97-119) filters the matched episodes down to those where `archimedesAttempted` is true, then computes `successes / attemptCount`, clamped to [0,1]. Episodes where Archimedes was never tried don't count.
- **`attemptCount`** — the number of those attempted episodes for the matched pattern.
- **The `min(1, attemptCount / 10)` factor** is a sample-size discount: it ramps linearly from 0.1 at 1 attempt to 1.0 at 10 attempts, then saturates. So a 100% success rate over 3 attempts yields confidence 0.30, not 1.0 — the gate is passed but the decision is reported as provisional until there are 10 attempts on that pattern.
- **`clamp01()`** (competence.ts:29-32) guards against NaN/Infinity and out-of-range values, returning 0 for non-finite input.

For contrast, the other two branches use different formulas:
- Below `minAttempts` (default 3): `0.3 + attemptCount/minAttempts × 0.3` → range [0.3, 0.6] (line 172).
- Below threshold (escalating): `(1 − successRate) × min(1, attemptCount/10)` — confidence in the *escalation* decision, so it rises as the success rate falls (line 191).
- Disabled config: hardcoded `1` (line 154); internal error: `0` (line 199).

Defaults from `types.ts:68-76`: `competenceThreshold: 0.7`, `minAttempts: 3`, so the "meets threshold" branch needs ≥3 attempts and ≥70% success, and confidence only reaches its full value at 10 attempts.

Found it. Here are the regex patterns used in `inferTaskCategory` (from line 127–134 of `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts`):

**For `'review'`:**
```regex
/\b(review|audit|lint|check)\b/
```
This matches task descriptions containing any of the words **review**, **audit**, **lint**, or **check** as whole words (case-insensitive, since `task.toLowerCase()` is called first).

**For `'research'`:**
```regex
/\b(research|explore|find|investigate|understand)\b/
```
This matches task descriptions containing any of the words **research**, **explore**, **find**, **investigate**, or **understand** as whole words.

The function checks these in order: first `'review'`, then `'research'`, then other categories like `refactor`, `implementation`, and falls back to `'other'` if none match.

Order of evaluation matters — first match wins:
1. `review` — review, audit, lint, check
2. `research` — research, explore, find, investigate, understand
3. `refactor` — refactor, restructure, rename, migrate (line 131)
4. `implementation` — implement, fix, add, write, create, build, update (line 132)
5. fallback `'other'` (line 133)

So a task like "review and investigate the parser" lands in `'review'`, not `'research'`, because the review check runs first.

## The patterns

`inferTaskCategory(task)` lowercases the task text and tests four regexes **in order**, returning the first match:

| Order | Category | Regex |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `review` | `/\b(review\|audit\|lint\|check)\b/` |
| 2 | `research` | `/\b(research\|explore\|find\|investigate\|understand)\b/` |
| 3 | **`refactor`** | `/\b(refactor\|restructure\|rename\|migrate)\b/` |
| 4 | **`implementation`** | `/\b(implement\|fix\|add\|write\|create\|build\|update)\b/` |
| — | `other` | fallback if nothing matches |

So the split you asked about is:

- **refactor** → `refactor`, `restructure`, `rename`, `migrate`
- **implementation** → `implement`, `fix`, `add`, `write`, `create`, `build`, `update`

## Two things worth knowing about how it behaves

**Order decides ties, not specificity.** Because `refactor` is tested before `implementation`, "refactor and add tests" is classified `refactor`. But `review` and `research` are tested *before* both, so "review the refactor" is `review` and "find and rename the helper" is `research`. The first keyword in the list wins, regardless of which one describes the actual work.

**Matching is whole-word and substring-based.** `\b` boundaries mean `refactoring` does **not** match `refactor` (the `\b` after `refactor` fails against the `i`), and `migrated` doesn't match `migrate`. Meanwhile `implement` matches inside `implementation` only via the `\b`-prefixed start — `implementation` does match, since `implement` is followed by `a`, which is a word char, so `\b` fails there too. In practice: inflected forms fall through to `other` unless another keyword is present.

The category type itself is declared at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:83` as `'research' | 'implementation' | 'review' | 'refactor' | 'other'`, and is used for competence reports and fine-tune bucketing — so a misclassification here skews those buckets rather than changing routing.

Now I have the full function. Let me summarize how `getCompetenceReport` computes the success rate for a category:

1. It takes an array of `episodes`.
2. It calls `safeEpisodes(episodes)` to get a list (presumably filtering out invalid entries).
3. It creates a Map called `buckets` where keys are task categories and values are objects `{ successes: number, count: number }`.
4. For each episode:
   - If `ep.archimedesAttempted` is falsy, it skips.
   - Gets the category from `ep.taskCategory ?? 'other'`.
   - Increments `b.count` by 1 for that bucket.
   - If `ep.archimedesSucceeded` is true, increments `b.successes` by 1.
5. After processing all episodes, it converts the map entries to an array of objects with keys: category, successRate, count.
6. The `successRate` is computed as `clamp01(count === 0 ? 0 : successes / count)`. So:
   - If count is 0 (i.e., no attempts for that category), successRate = 0.
   - Otherwise, successRate = successes / count (a ratio).
7. The array is sorted by `count` descending.

The function returns an array of `{ category: string; successRate: number; count: number }`, sorted so categories with more total episodes come first.

Let me also check what `clamp01` does — but I don't need to read that unless necessary. The key point is the formula for success rate.

So, to answer the question directly:

For each category:
- It counts how many times `ep.archimedesAttempted` was true (or attempted) → `count`.
- It counts how many of those had `ep.archimedesSucceeded === true` → `successes`.
- successRate = successes / count, but clamped to 0 if count is 0.

**Answer — `getCompetenceReport` (aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:260-286)**

It's a simple per-category ratio of successes to attempts, computed in one pass:

1. **Sanitize input** (line 264 → `safeEpisodes`, line 90): non-arrays become `[]`; episodes are kept only if they're objects with a string `task` and a boolean `archimedesAttempted`. Anything malformed is dropped before counting.
2. **Filter to real attempts** (line 268): episodes where `archimedesAttempted` is false are skipped entirely — they don't count as successes *or* failures, so a category's denominator is "times Archimedes actually tried", not "times the task appeared".
3. **Bucket by category** (lines 269-273): key is `ep.taskCategory`, defaulting to `'other'` when null/undefined. Each bucket accumulates `count += 1` per attempt and `successes += 1` when `ep.archimedesSucceeded` is truthy.
4. **Compute the rate** (line 279): `successRate = clamp01(count === 0 ? 0 : successes / count)` — successes ÷ attempts, clamped to [0,1] by `clamp01` (line 29), which also maps `NaN`/`Infinity` to 0. The `count === 0` guard is defensive only: a bucket can't exist with zero count, since buckets are created on the first increment.
5. **Sort** (line 282): descending by `count` (most-attempted categories first). Ties keep insertion order, i.e. first-seen order in the episode list.
6. **Never throws** (lines 263, 283-285): the whole body is wrapped in `try/catch` returning `[]`.

Two behavioural details worth knowing:
- The rate is **unweighted and un-smoothed** — a category with 1 attempt and 1 success reports `1.0`, identical to one with 100/100. If this feeds a trust decision (it's consumed by `src/doctor/checks.ts:14`), small-sample categories look maximally competent.
- `archimedesSucceeded` is only read when `archimedesAttempted` is true, so a "succeeded but never attempted" episode is silently ignored rather than counted as a success.

Complexity: O(n) time over episodes, O(k) space for k distinct categories.

Each `TrainingExample` produced by `generateTrainingData` (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/training-data.ts:35-47`) carries a `metadata` object with exactly four fields:

| Field | Source | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `projectRoot` | `ep.projectRoot` | Which project the episode came from. |
| `taskCategory` | `ep.taskCategory` | Category of the task. |
| `archimedesFailureReason` | derived from `ep.archimedesOutput` | Optional in the type. Set to `` `Archimedes output insufficient: ${ep.archimedesOutput.slice(0, 200)}` `` when Archimedes produced output (truncated to 200 chars), otherwise the literal `'Archimedes did not produce an acceptable result'`. |
| `timestamp` | `ep.timestamp` | Numeric (ms) timestamp of the episode. |

The type is declared at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:154-160`, where `archimedesFailureReason` is the only optional member.

Two things worth knowing about how these are used:

- **The metadata is not exported.** `exportJSONL` (`training-data.ts:59-80`) writes only `messages: [{role:'user', content: instruction + input}, {role:'assistant', content: output}]` — the metadata is dropped from the JSONL, so it exists purely for in-memory filtering/QA of rows, not for the fine-tuning payload.
- **The top-level `instruction` is shared, not per-row.** It's built once before the loop (`training-data.ts:17-26`) from `perception.trajectory.vision` (default `'Complete coding tasks accurately.'`) plus `perception.constraints.strictRules` joined with `'; '`; only `input` and `output` vary per example.

Rows are only emitted for episodes where `archimedesAttempted` is true, `archimedesSucceeded` is false, `largeModelOutput` is non-empty after trim, and `reviewerApproved` is true (`training-data.ts:31-33`).

I found the relevant code in `/aura-code-src/src/archimedes/training-data.ts`, lines 30–33. Here's what determines whether an episode is **skipped** (via `continue`) during training data generation:

```typescript
for (const ep of episodes) {
  if (!ep?.archimedesAttempted || ep.archimedesSucceeded) continue;
  if (!ep.largeModelOutput?.trim()) continue;
  if (!ep.reviewerApproved) continue;

  // ... push example ...
}
```

An episode is **skipped** when **any** of the following conditions are true:

| # | Condition (skip if TRUE) | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `!ep.archimedesAttempted` — Archimedes did not attempt this task, or attempted but no record exists | No attempt was made by Archimedes |
| 2 | `ep.archimedesSucceeded` — Archimedes succeeded on the episode | The model already produced a correct result; no need to generate training data from it |
| 3 | `!ep.largeModelOutput?.trim()` — Large model output is empty or whitespace-only (no trimmed content) | No valid large-model output to use as ground truth |
| 4 | `!ep.reviewerApproved` — The reviewer did not approve the episode's result | The human reviewer rejected/flagged the output |

**In short:** An episode is included only when **all three** conditions are met:
- Archimedes attempted (`archimedesAttempted` is truthy) but **did NOT succeed** (`archimedesSucceeded` is falsy),
- Large model produced non-empty trimmed output (`largeModelOutput?.trim()` exists and has content), and
- The reviewer approved the result (`reviewerApproved` is truthy).

If any of those four checks fails, `continue` skips that episode.

I found the relevant code in `/tmp/aura-bench/session_11018/t25-04/aura-code-src/src/archimedes/training-data.ts`. The function `exportJSONL` (lines 59–80) handles writing training data to disk. Here's how it avoids corruption:

### Steps in `exportJSONL`:

1. **Ensures the output directory exists** — creates it with `fs.mkdirSync(dir, { recursive: true })` if missing.

2. **Writes to a temporary file first** (line 78):
   ```ts
   const tmp = outputPath + '.tmp';
   await fs.promises.writeFile(tmp, lines.join('\n') + (lines.length ? '\n' : ''), 'utf8');
   ```
   This writes the JSONL content atomically to a `.tmp` file before doing anything else.

3. **Renames (moves) the temp file to the final destination** (line 79):
   ```ts
   await fs.promises.rename(tmp, outputPath);
   ```

### Why this avoids corruption:
- The standard `fs.writeFile(path)` can leave a partially-written file on disk if interrupted.
- By writing to **`outputPath + '.tmp'`** first and then using `fs.rename()` (which is an atomic rename on the same filesystem), there's never a window where the target path holds incomplete data. The final write completes atomically, so readers see either the complete file or nothing — no half-written rows.

This is the classic **write-to-temp-then-rename** pattern for safe cross-device and crash-safe writes.

# How `fineTuneWithOllama` builds the custom system instructions

File: `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts`, function at **line 29**, Modelfile construction at **lines 43–68**.

Ollama has no native fine-tune API, so this function fakes specialization by baking training examples into the model's **system prompt** via a generated `Modelfile`. It happens in four steps:

**1. Read and count the JSONL (lines 39–41)**
The training file is read as text, split on newlines, blank lines dropped. `trainingExamples` is just the line count — the total number of examples, not the number used.

**2. Extract snippets from the first 50 examples (lines 43–55)**
```ts
for (const line of lines.slice(0, 50)) {
  const row = JSON.parse(line) as JsonlRow;
  const user = row.messages?.find(m => m.role === 'user')?.content ?? '';
  const assistant = row.messages?.find(m => m.role === 'assistant')?.content ?? '';
  if (user || assistant) snippets.push(`User: ${user}\nAssistant: ${assistant}`);
}
```
- Only the **first 50 lines** are used — a hard cap to keep the system prompt inside the context window.
- Each row is parsed as OpenAI-style JSONL (`{ messages: [{role, content}] }`); the first `user` and first `assistant` message are pulled out (system messages are ignored).
- Each pair is flattened into a plain `User: …\nAssistant: …` block.
- Malformed lines are silently skipped by the `catch {}` — they don't abort the run.

**3. Assemble the system block (lines 57–61)**
```ts
const systemBlock = [
  'You are a specialized coding assistant.',
  'Learn from these corrected examples where a larger model intervened:',
  ...snippets,
].join('\n\n');
```
A fixed two-line preamble, then every snippet, all joined with blank lines. So the "custom instructions" are: a role statement + an instruction to imitate the examples + the examples themselves.

**4. Emit the Modelfile (lines 63–68)**
```ts
const modelfile = [
  `FROM ${baseModel}`,
  'SYSTEM """',
  systemBlock.replace(/"""/g, "'''"),
  '"""',
].join('\n');
```
- `FROM <baseModel>` inherits the base weights.
- The system block goes inside a triple-quoted `SYSTEM """…"""` block.
- Any literal `"""` inside the training text is rewritten to `'''` so it can't terminate the block early — the one escaping measure taken.

**Then (lines 70–76)** the Modelfile is written to a fresh temp dir (`os.tmpdir()/aura-ft-*`) and `ollama create <outputModelName> -f <modelfilePath>` is run with a 300 s timeout. Success returns `status: 'completed'`; any throw is caught and returned as `status: 'failed'` with `error: String(e)` — the function never throws.

**Two things worth knowing about this design:**
- It is **not** fine-tuning. No weights change; the examples are prompt context, so they consume tokens on every inference and can be overridden by the base model's own behavior.
- The `replace(/"""/g, "'''")` guard is the only sanitization — a snippet containing `'''` is harmless, but nothing strips other Modelfile directives, and the 50-example cap means a large dataset is silently truncated while `trainingExamples` still reports the full count.

Found it. In `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts` at line 70:

```typescript
const tmpDir = await fs.promises.mkdtemp(path.join(os.tmpdir(), 'aura-ft-'));
```

And the Modelfile path is then written to:
```typescript
const modelfilePath = path.join(tmpDir, 'Modelfile');
```

So the temporary directory for generating the Modelfile is created via `fs.promises.mkdtemp` at:

**`<os.tmpdir()>/aura-ft-<random UUID>`**

That is, under the system's default temp directory (typically `/tmp`) with a subdirectory named `aura-ft-`, followed by a unique identifier generated from `randomUUID()`. The full path to the Modelfile then becomes `<that tmpDir>/Modelfile`.

For example: `/tmp/aura-ft-<uuid>/Modelfile`.

```ts
const tmpDir = await fs.promises.mkdtemp(path.join(os.tmpdir(), 'aura-ft-'));
```

The Modelfile is written into that directory at line 71–72 (`path.join(tmpDir, 'Modelfile')`), then passed to `ollama create <model> -f <modelfilePath>` at line 74.

So the location is the OS temp directory (`os.tmpdir()` — `/tmp` on Linux) in a uniquely-named subdirectory with the prefix `aura-ft-`, e.g. `/tmp/aura-ft-a1b2c3/Modelfile`.

One thing worth flagging: that temp directory is never cleaned up — there's no `fs.rm(tmpDir)` after the `ollama create` call or in the `catch` block, so each fine-tune run leaves a `aura-ft-*` directory behind.

```typescript
if (!apiKey) {
```

In `/aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts`, lines 32–34 describe the `Permissions` option for an alternator session:

> **The session's permission system. When omitted, defaults to the safe `'normal'` level — NEVER `'auto'`: the Archimedes attempt must not auto-approve destructive operations the user's chosen mode would have prompted for.**

So when no custom `permissions` object is passed (i.e., it's undefined/omitted), the default permission level is **`'normal'`** — and it will **never be `'auto'`**.

This means Archimedes attempts must not auto-approve destructive operations; they require user confirmation as intended.

**Answer: The default permission level of an alternator session when no custom `permissions` object is passed is `'normal'` (safe), never `'auto'`.**

**Answer: confidence = `successRate × min(1, attemptCount / 10)`, clamped to [0, 1].**

That's the whole formula, at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:182`:

```ts
confidence: clamp01(level.successRate * Math.min(1, level.attemptCount / 10)),
```

### How it gets there

1. **Success rate** (`competence.ts:101-104`) — only episodes where Archimedes actually attempted count:
   `successRate = successes / attemptCount`, and `0` if there were no attempts.
2. **Gate** (`:168`) — if `attemptCount < minAttempts` (default **3**), the threshold branch is never reached; you get the learning-phase confidence `0.3 + (attemptCount / minAttempts) × 0.3` (`:172`).
3. **Threshold branch** (`:178-186`) — once `successRate >= competenceThreshold` (default **0.7**), Archimedes is trusted and confidence is the product above.

### What the formula actually means

The second factor is a **sample-size discount**: it ramps linearly from 0.1 at 1 attempt to 1.0 at 10 attempts, then saturates. So a high success rate is not taken at face value until there is evidence behind it.

| attempts | successRate | confidence |
|---|---|---|
| 3 | 1.00 | 0.30 |
| 5 | 1.00 | 0.50 |
| 10 | 1.00 | 1.00 |
| 10 | 0.70 (just at threshold) | 0.70 |
| 20 | 0.70 | 0.70 |

Two consequences worth knowing:

- **Confidence can never exceed the success rate** — the multiplier is capped at 1. A pattern sitting exactly on the 0.7 threshold is reported as 0.7 confidence no matter how many attempts it has.
- **The floor is 0.3, not 0** — because `minAttempts` (3) is below the discount's saturation point (10), the lowest confidence you can see in the threshold branch is `0.7 × 0.3 = 0.21`. The 0.1–0.2 range is unreachable in practice.

### The mirror case (for contrast)

When the rate is *below* threshold (`:191`), confidence is `(1 - successRate) × min(1, attemptCount / 10)` — the same sample-size discount applied to the *confidence that escalating is the right call*. So the two branches are symmetric: one measures confidence in trusting the small model, the other in overriding it.

Also note `clamp01` (`:29-32`) returns `0` for `NaN`/`Infinity`, so a corrupt episode list degrades to zero confidence rather than propagating a bad number — and the outer `try/catch` (`:195`) falls back to `useArchimedes: true, confidence: 0`.

Found it. Here are the regex patterns in `inferTaskCategory` (lines 127–134 of `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts`):

**For `'review'` category — line 129:**
```regex
/\b(review|audit|lint|check)\b/
```
This matches the whole-word tokens **review**, **audit**, **lint**, or **check**. If `task.toLowerCase()` contains any of these, it returns `'review'`.

**For `'research'` category — line 130:**
```regex
/\b(research|explore|find|investigate|understand)\b/
```
This matches the whole-word tokens **research**, **explore**, **find**, **investigate**, or **understand**. If `task.toLowerCase()` contains any of these (and not one from the review list), it returns `'research'`.

**Key details:**
- Both patterns use word boundaries (`\b`) so partial matches don't trigger.
- The checks are evaluated in order: first 'review', then 'research', then 'refactor', etc. So a task containing both "audit" and "investigate" would be classified as `'review'` (since review is checked first).
- If none match, it falls through to the default return of `'other'`.

Found it. Here are the regex patterns in `inferTaskCategory` (from line 127–134 of `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts`):

```typescript
function inferTaskCategory(task: string): TaskCategory {
  const t = task.toLowerCase();
  if (/\b(review|audit|lint|check)\b/.test(t)) return 'review';
  if (/\b(research|explore|find|investigate|understand)\b/.test(t)) return 'research';
  // ...
}
```

**For categorizing as `'review'`:**
The regex `/\b(review|audit|lint|check)\b/` — it matches the whole-word tokens **review**, **audit**, **lint**, or **check**. If any of these appear in the task (case-insensitive, since input is lowercased first), it returns `'review'`.

**For categorizing as `'research'`:**
The regex `/\b(research|explore|find|investigate|understand)\b/` — it matches whole-word tokens **research**, **explore**, **find**, **investigate**, or **understand**. If any of these appear, it returns `'research'`.

Note: The checks are evaluated in order. So if a task contains both "review" and "research", the first matching pattern (`/review|audit|lint|check/`) takes precedence and returns `'review'`.

I found the exact source. Here's what I discovered in `archimedes/alternator.ts` (lines 127–134):

```typescript
function inferTaskCategory(task: string): TaskCategory {
  const t = task.toLowerCase();
  if (/\b(review|audit|lint|check)\b/.test(t)) return 'review';
  if (/\b(research|explore|find|investigate|understand)\b/.test(t)) return 'research';
  if (/\b(refactor|restructure|rename|migrate)\b/.test(t)) return 'refactor';
  if (/\b(implement|fix|add|write|create|build|update)\b/.test(t)) return 'implementation';
  return 'other';
}
```

### The two regex patterns

**`'refactor'`** — line 131:
```regex
/\\b(refactor|restructure|rename|migrate)\\b/
```
Matches any task whose lowercase text contains one of: `refactor`, `restructure`, `rename`, or `migrate` as a whole word.

**`'implementation'`** — line 132:
```regex
/\\b(implement|fix|add|write|create|build|update)\\b/
```
Matches any task whose lowercase text contains one of: `implement`, `fix`, `add`, `write`, `create`, `build`, or `update` as a whole word.

### How it fits into the full pipeline

- The function is called **`inferTaskCategory(task)`** from within the **ArchimedesAlternator** loop (see line 131 context in `alternator.ts`).
- It returns a value of type **`TaskCategory = 'research' | 'implementation' | 'review' | 'refactor' | 'other'`** (defined at line 83).
- The categories drive routing and task dispatch — e.g., research tasks go to the *architect* mode for planning, implementation tasks get handed off to specialists or execution tools.

### Quick reference table

| Category      | Keywords matched by regex |
|---------------|---------------------------|
| `research`    | `research`, `explore`, `find`, `investigate`, `understand` |
| `review`      | `review`, `audit`, `lint`, `check` |
| **`refactor`** | **`refactor`, `restructure`, `rename`, `migrate`** |
| **`implementation`** | **`implement`, `fix`, `add`, `write`, `create`, `build`, `update`** |
| **other**     | (default fallback) |

So in short: **refactor vs. implementation is decided by whether the task text contains any of those specific keywords**, using word-boundary anchors (`\b`) to avoid partial matches.

  ```typescript
function inferTaskCategory(task: string): TaskCategory {

    const t = task.toLowerCase();
    if (/\b(review|audit|lint|check)\b/.test(t)) return 'review';
    if (/\b(research|explore|find|investigate|understand)\b/.test(t)) return 'research';
    if (/\b(refactor|restructure|rename|migrate)\b/.test(t)) return 'refactor';
    if (/\b(implement|fix|add|write|create|build|update)\b/.test(t)) return 'implementation';
    return 'other';
  }
  ```
  
  When a task's text does **not** match any of the four regex patterns (`review`, `audit`, `lint`, `check`; `research`, `explore`, `find`, `investigate`, `understand`; `refactor`, `restructure`, `rename`, `migrate`; or `implement`, `fix`, `add`, `write`, `create`, `build`, `update`), the function falls through to the final line and returns **`'other'`**.

Here's how **`getCompetenceReport`** computes the success rate for each category:

### Algorithm (from `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts`, lines 260–285)

1. **Group episodes by task category.** It iterates over the input `episodes` via `safeEpisodes(episodes)`, which filters out invalid entries.
2. For each episode:
   - Skips it if `ep.archimedesAttempted` is falsy (no attempt recorded).
   - Determines the category: `cat = ep.taskCategory ?? 'other'` (falls back to `'other'` if no category is set).
   - Increments `b.count` by 1 for that category.
   - If `ep.archimedesSucceeded` is true, increments `b.successes` by 1.
3. After processing all episodes, it builds the result array from the map entries:
   ```js
   .map(([category, { successes, count }]) => ({
       category,
       successRate: clamp01(count === 0 ? 0 : successes / count),
       count,
   }))
   ```
4. **Success rate formula:** `successes / count` — i.e., the ratio of successful attempts to total attempts for that category.
5. **`clamp01`**: a helper (not shown in this snippet) clamps the result so it never falls outside `[0, 1]`. If `count === 0`, successRate is forced to `0`.
6. The final array is **sorted by `count` descending** (most attempts first).

### Summary
For each category, it tallies how many episodes had `archimedesSucceeded = true` (`successes`) and the total number of attempted episodes (`count`), then computes:

```
successRate = 0            if count == 0
             else successes / count
```

and returns `{ category, successRate, count }` for every non-empty bucket.

I found the relevant code in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts`. Here's how `getCompetenceReport` sorts the output array:

The function builds a `Map<string, { successes: number; count: number }>()` keyed by category (from each episode's `taskCategory`, defaulting to `'other'`). It then returns an array derived from `buckets.entries()`, maps over it into objects `{ category, successRate, count }`, and finally applies:

```javascript
.sort((a, b) => b.count - a.count);
```

**Categories are sorted by `count` (the number of episodes in that category) in descending order — i.e., the most frequent categories appear first.**

So if two categories have different counts, the one with the higher count comes earlier. If counts are equal, the original insertion order from the Map is preserved as a tiebreaker.

In short: **primary sort key = `count` (descending).**

**`getCompetenceReport`** — `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:260-286`

The success rate is a plain per-category ratio of successes to attempts, computed in one pass:

1. **Sanitize input** (line 264): `safeEpisodes()` (line 90) drops anything that isn't an array and filters out entries that aren't objects with a string `task` and a boolean `archimedesAttempted`. Non-array input → empty report.
2. **Bucket by category** (lines 267-274): for each episode, skip it entirely if `archimedesAttempted` is falsy — so the denominator is *attempts*, not all episodes. The key is `ep.taskCategory ?? 'other'`, so uncategorized episodes fall into a literal `"other"` bucket (which will merge with any real category named `"other"`). Each bucket accumulates `count += 1` and `successes += 1` when `ep.archimedesSucceeded` is truthy.
3. **Compute the rate** (line 279): `clamp01(count === 0 ? 0 : successes / count)`. The `count === 0` guard is unreachable in practice — a bucket only exists because at least one episode incremented it — but it keeps the division safe. `clamp01` (line 29) maps non-finite values to `0` and otherwise pins the result to `[0, 1]`.
4. **Order** (line 282): sorted by `count` descending, so the most-exercised categories come first. Ties keep insertion order (stable sort).

Two behavioral details worth knowing: the rate is a raw fraction (`0.75`), not a percentage, and it is **not** weighted by anything else — a category with 1 success out of 1 attempt reports `1.0` and sorts below a category with 50/100. The whole function is wrapped in `try/catch` returning `[]`, so it never throws.

Complexity is O(n) over episodes plus O(k log k) for the sort over k distinct categories.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

Each bucket accumulates `count += 1` and `successes += 1` when `ep.archimedesSucceeded` is truthy.
  3. **Compute the rate** (line 279): `clamp01(count === 0 ? 0 : successes / count)`. The `count === 0` guard is unreachable in practice — a bucket only exists because at least one episode incremented it — but it keeps the division safe. `clamp01` (line 29) maps non-finite values to `0` and otherwise pins the result to `[0, 1]`.
  4. **Order** (line 282): sorted by `count` descending, so the most-exercised categories come first. Ties keep insertion order (stable sort).

**Answer:** When `display` is omitted from `AlternatorOptions`, the alternator falls back to a **no-op display** — a silent stub that swallows every UI event.

The mechanism, in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts`:

- **Line 30** — `display?: Display;` is optional in the options interface.
- **Line 279** — the constructor does `this.display = opts.display ?? createNoopDisplay();`
- **Lines 100–121** — `createNoopDisplay()` returns a `Display` object where all 19 methods are empty functions: `agentThinking`, `streamText`, `streamEnd`, `toolStart`, `toolCall`, `toolResult`, `toolBlocked`, `warning`, `success`, `error`, `header`, `summary`, `showPlan`, `stepStarted`, `stepCompleted`, `contextBar`, `contextDashboard`, `compactionEvent`.

Practical effect: the alternator still runs identically — routing decisions, Archimedes attempts, escalation to the large model, episode recording — but nothing is printed. Every `this.display.header(...)`, `.warning(...)`, `.success(...)`, `.error(...)` call at lines 345–502 becomes a no-op. This is what makes the class safe to embed in non-interactive callers (bots, pipelines, tests) that have no terminal UI. The same stub is also threaded into both inner agent loops (lines 366 and 448), so the sub-agents are silent too.

Note the contrast with the neighbouring option: `permissions` (line 280) defaults to `new PermissionSystem('normal')` — deliberately *not* `'auto'`, so a missing display never implies a missing safety gate.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  - **Line 30** — `display?: Display;` is optional in the options interface.
  - **Line 279** — the constructor does `this.display = opts.display ?? createNoopDisplay();`
  - **Lines 100–121** — `createNoopDisplay()` returns a `Display` object where all 19 methods are empty functions: `agentThinking`, `streamText`, `streamEnd`, `toolStart`, `toolCall`, `toolResult`, `toolBlocked`, `warning`, `success`, `error`, `header`, `summary`, `showPlan`, `stepStarted`, `stepCompleted`, `contextBar`, `contextDashboard`, `compactionEvent`.

Every `this.display.header(...)`, `.warning(...)`, `.success(...)`, `.error(...)` call at lines 345–502 becomes a no-op. **`disableSpawn`** — set to `true` at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:367`.

It's passed into the `runAgentLoop({...})` call that runs the Archimedes attempt (lines 357–373). It's a field on `AgentLoopOptions` in `aura-code-src/src/agent/loop.ts:54`, documented as "Disables subagent tool entirely (e.g. for tests)" — so when `true`, the `spawn_task` tool is removed from the loop and Archimedes cannot spawn nested agent loops.

Two related details worth knowing:

- The sibling gate is `spawnConfig` (`loop.ts:51–52`): if it's undefined, `spawn_task` returns an error. So spawning needs both `disableSpawn` falsy **and** a `spawnConfig` present.
- The alternator hardcodes `disableSpawn: true` — it is not exposed on `AlternatorOptions`, so callers can't turn it back on. The same hardcoded disable appears in `aura-code-src/src/research/council.ts:244`.

Each `TrainingExample` produced by `generateTrainingData` (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/training-data.ts:35-47`) carries a `metadata` object with exactly four fields:

| Field | Source | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `projectRoot` | `ep.projectRoot` | Absolute path to the project root |
| `taskCategory` | `ep.taskCategory` | One of `research` / `implementation` / `review` / `refactor` / `other` |
| `archimedesFailureReason` | derived from `ep.archimedesOutput` | `"Archimedes output insufficient: <first 200 chars>"` if Archimedes produced output, otherwise the literal `"Archimedes did not produce an acceptable result"` |
| `timestamp` | `ep.timestamp` | Unix ms when the episode completed |

The shape is declared in `TrainingExample.metadata` at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:154-160`, where `archimedesFailureReason` is optional (though this function always sets it).

Two things worth knowing, since they affect what actually lands in the data:

- **The metadata is dropped on export.** `exportJSONL` (`training-data.ts:66-75`) writes only `messages: [{role:'user'}, {role:'assistant'}]` — `instruction` and `input` are concatenated into the user turn, and the whole `metadata` block is never serialized. So these fields exist in memory only; they are not in the JSONL file.
- **Rows are filtered before metadata is built** (`training-data.ts:31-33`): only episodes where Archimedes was attempted, did *not* succeed, produced a non-empty `largeModelOutput`, and were `reviewerApproved` become examples. Everything else is skipped, and any thrown error is swallowed into an empty array (`:51-53`).

For each episode (`ep`), the function checks three conditions, and if **any one is false**, it skips to the next episode:

I found the exact construction. Here's how the `instruction` field of a `TrainingExample` is built inside `generateTrainingData()` in **`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/training-data.ts`** (lines 17–26):

```typescript
const vision = perception?.trajectory?.vision ?? 'Complete coding tasks accurately.';
const instruction = [
    'You are a specialized coding assistant for this project.',
    vision,
    perception?.constraints?.strictRules?.length
      ? `Constraints: ${perception.constraints.strictRules.join('; ')}`
      : '',
]
  .filter(Boolean)
  .join(' ');
```

**Step-by-step breakdown:**

1. **`vision`** — pulled from `perception.trajectory.vision`. If that property is missing on the perception object, it falls back to the default string: `"Complete coding tasks accurately."`

2. **Array elements (3 items):**
   - Index 0: `'You are a specialized coding assistant for this project.'` — a fixed system prompt.
   - Index 1: `vision` from step 1 above.
   - Index 2: A conditional string:
     - If `perception.constraints.strictRules` exists and has **at least one** element (`strictRules?.length > 0`), the third element is `"Constraints: " + strictRules joined by `'; ''` (e.g., `"Constraints: Always follow style guide."`).
     - Otherwise, the third element is an empty string `''`.

3. **`filter(Boolean)`** — removes any falsy values from the array (so if `strictRules` were somehow empty or undefined and produced a falsy result, it would be dropped).

4. **`.join(' ')`** — joins all remaining elements with a single space to produce the final instruction string.

In short:  
> *instruction = "You are a specialized coding assistant for this project. [vision] [Constraints: strictRules joined by ; ] (if any)"*  

with extra constraints appended only when `perception.constraints.strictRules` is non-empty, otherwise just the first two parts.

## How the `instruction` string is built

**Location:** `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/training-data.ts`, lines 17–26, inside `generateTrainingData()`.

It is built **once per call**, not per example — every `TrainingExample` produced by a single run shares the identical `instruction` string. It is a three-part, space-joined, empty-part-filtered concatenation:

```ts
const vision = perception?.trajectory?.vision ?? 'Complete coding tasks accurately.';
const instruction = [
  'You are a specialized coding assistant for this project.',   // 1. fixed role preamble
  vision,                                                        // 2. project vision
  perception?.constraints?.strictRules?.length                   // 3. optional constraints
    ? `Constraints: ${perception.constraints.strictRules.join('; ')}`
    : '',
]
  .filter(Boolean)   // drops the empty constraints slot
  .join(' ');        // single space between parts
```

**The three parts:**

1. **Fixed preamble** — the literal `"You are a specialized coding assistant for this project."` Always present.
2. **Project vision** — `perception.trajectory.vision`, falling back to `"Complete coding tasks accurately."` when perception is missing or `vision` is undefined (optional chaining on both hops, so a null `perception` is safe).
3. **Constraints** — only when `perception.constraints.strictRules` is a non-empty array; rendered as `Constraints: rule1; rule2; rule3` (semicolon-space joined). Otherwise the slot is `''` and `.filter(Boolean)` removes it, so no double space or dangling separator.

**What is *not* in the instruction:** the task itself. `ep.task` goes into the separate `input` field (line 37), and the target goes into `output` (line 38). The instruction is purely the static system-level framing.

**Downstream:** `exportJSONL()` (lines 66–75) recombines them as `userContent = \`${ex.instruction}\n\n${ex.input}\`` — so the instruction becomes the head of the user message, blank-line separated from the task, with `ex.output` as the assistant turn. `fineTuneWithOllama()` then reads those rows back and folds the first 50 into a Modelfile `SYSTEM` block.

**Two things worth flagging:**

- **The `perception` type is dangling.** `training-data.ts:3` imports `ProjectPerception` from `../perception/types.js`, but `src/perception/` contains only `index.ts` — there is no `types.ts`, and `index.ts:4` re-exports from that same missing file. `trajectory` and `constraints` appear nowhere else in the tree. So the vision and constraints branches are effectively dead in this snapshot: the type doesn't resolve, and nothing in the codebase ever populates those fields. In practice the instruction collapses to the preamble plus the fallback vision string.
- **The whole function is wrapped in a bare `catch { return []; }`** (lines 14, 51–53), so any failure — including a malformed `perception` — silently yields zero training examples rather than surfacing an error.

**Answer: it writes to a temp file, then atomically renames it over the target.**

The write happens in `exportJSONL()` at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/training-data.ts:59-80`:

1. **Directory first** (lines 63-64) — `mkdirSync(dir, { recursive: true })` if the parent dir is missing, so the write can't fail on a nonexistent path.
2. **Serialize to memory** (lines 66-75) — every example becomes one OpenAI-style JSONL line (`{"messages":[{"role":"user",...},{"role":"assistant",...}]}`), joined with `\n` and given a trailing newline only when non-empty (line 78). Nothing touches disk until the whole payload is built.
3. **Write to a sibling temp file** (lines 77-78) — `outputPath + '.tmp'`, written with `await fs.promises.writeFile(...)`. The `await` matters: the promise resolves only after the file is fully flushed, so the rename can't race a half-written buffer.
4. **Atomic rename** (line 79) — `await fs.promises.rename(tmp, outputPath)`. On POSIX this is an atomic `rename(2)` within the same filesystem, so a reader either sees the complete old file or the complete new one — never a truncated or partially-written JSONL.

That's the corruption guard: a crash or kill mid-write leaves a stray `.tmp` file and the previous good `outputPath` intact, rather than a half-written dataset that would silently break a fine-tune run.

Two honest caveats about the current implementation:

- **No `fsync` before the rename.** The rename is atomic with respect to other processes, but on a power loss the renamed file's *contents* aren't guaranteed durable — the metadata can land before the data. For a regenerable training set that's an acceptable trade; for irreplaceable data you'd `fs.open` → `write` → `fsync` → `close` → `rename`.
- **`exportJSONL` is exported but has no callers** in `src/` — it's re-exported from `archimedes/index.ts:18` and nothing else invokes it. So the atomic-write path is correct but currently unexercised; `generateTrainingData` (lines 10-54) only builds the array in memory and returns it.

  1. **Directory first** (lines 63-64) — `mkdirSync(dir, { recursive: true })` if the parent dir is missing, so the write can't fail on a nonexistent path.
  2. **Serialize to memory** (lines 66-75) — every example becomes one OpenAI-style JSONL line (`{"messages":[{"role":"user",...},{"role":"assistant",...}]}`), joined with `\n` and given a trailing newline only when non-empty (line 78). Nothing touches disk until the whole payload is built.
  3. **Write to a sibling temp file** (lines 77-78) — `outputPath + '.tmp'`, written with `await fs.promises.writeFile(...)`. The `await` matters: the promise resolves only after the file is fully flushed, so the rename can't race a half-written buffer.
  4. **Atomic rename** (line 79) — `await fs.promises.rename(tmp, outputPath)`. On POSIX this is an atomic `rename(2)` within the same filesystem, so a reader either sees the complete old file or the complete new one — never a truncated or partially-written JSONL.
  
  That's the corruption guard: a crash or kill mid-write leaves a stray `.tmp` file and the previous good `outputPath` intact, rather than a half-written dataset that would silently break a fine-tune run.

I have the full picture. Here's how it works.

## `fineTuneWithOllama` — how the system instructions are built

File: `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts`, lines 29–99.

Ollama has no fine-tuning API, so the function fakes specialization by baking the training examples into a **`SYSTEM` block of a generated Modelfile** and calling `ollama create`. The system instructions are assembled in four steps:

**1. Read and count the JSONL (lines 39–41)**
The training file is read as text, split on newlines, blank lines dropped, and the remaining line count becomes `trainingExamples`.

**2. Extract up to 50 user/assistant pairs (lines 43–55)**
For each of the first 50 lines it parses the row as `{ messages: [{role, content}] }`, pulls the first `user` message and the first `assistant` message, and formats them as:

```
User: <user content>
Assistant: <assistant content>
```

Rows that fail to parse are silently skipped (`catch { /* skip bad line */ }`), and rows where both fields are empty are dropped too — so `trainingExamples` (raw line count) can exceed the number of snippets actually embedded.

**3. Compose the system block (lines 57–61)**
A fixed preamble plus the snippets, joined with blank lines:

```
You are a specialized coding assistant.

Learn from these corrected examples where a larger model intervened:

User: …
Assistant: …

User: …
Assistant: …
```

**4. Emit the Modelfile (lines 63–68)**
```
FROM <baseModel>
SYSTEM """
<systemBlock, with any """ replaced by '''>
"""
```
The `.replace(/"""/g, "'''")` is the escaping guard: Ollama's Modelfile uses triple-quoted strings, so any `"""` inside the training text would terminate the block early and corrupt the file — it's swapped for `'''` instead.

**Then (lines 70–76):** the Modelfile is written to a fresh temp dir (`os.tmpdir()/aura-ft-*`), and `ollama create <outputModelName> -f <modelfilePath>` runs with a 300 s timeout. Success returns a `completed` `FineTuneJob`; any throw (missing file, `ollama` not installed, create failure) is caught and returned as `failed` with `error: String(e)` — the function never throws.

**Two things worth knowing:** the temp dir is never cleaned up, and the "fine-tune" is really prompt-stuffing — the base model's weights are untouched, so the examples consume context window on every inference rather than being learned.

**Answer — `fineTuneWithOllama` (aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:29-99)**

Ollama has no fine-tune API, so the function fakes specialization by baking training examples into a Modelfile's `SYSTEM` block. The construction happens in three steps:

**1. Read and count the JSONL (lines 39-41)**
It reads the training file as text, splits on newlines, drops blank lines, and uses the line count as `trainingExamples`.

**2. Extract example snippets (lines 43-55)**
For the **first 50 lines only** (`lines.slice(0, 50)`), each line is `JSON.parse`d as `{ messages: [{role, content}] }`. It pulls the first `user` message and the first `assistant` message, and if either is non-empty, pushes a formatted pair:
```
User: <user content>
Assistant: <assistant content>
```
Malformed lines are silently skipped (`catch { /* skip bad line */ }`) — so a bad line is dropped, never invented or repaired.

**3. Build the system block (lines 57-61)**
A fixed two-line preamble plus the snippets, joined with blank lines between every element:
```
You are a specialized coding assistant.

Learn from these corrected examples where a larger model intervened:

User: ...
Assistant: ...

User: ...
Assistant: ...
```

**4. Emit the Modelfile (lines 63-68)**
```
FROM <baseModel>
SYSTEM """
<systemBlock, with any """ replaced by '''>
"""
```
The `.replace(/"""/g, "'''")` is the important detail: because the block is wrapped in a triple-quoted `SYSTEM """..."""` heredoc, any literal `"""` inside the training text would terminate the string early and corrupt the Modelfile. It's swapped for `'''` to keep the file parseable.

**5. Write and create (lines 70-76)**
The Modelfile is written to a fresh temp dir (`mkdtemp(os.tmpdir()/aura-ft-)`), then `ollama create <outputModelName> -f <modelfilePath>` runs with a 300s timeout. Success returns `status: 'completed'`; any throw (missing file, ollama not installed, create failure) is caught and returned as `status: 'failed'` with `error: String(e)` — the function never throws.

Two things worth flagging as real limitations, not bugs: only 50 examples ever reach the system prompt regardless of dataset size, and the whole thing is prompt-stuffing, not weight training — the base model is unchanged.

Details that matter:

- **No native fine-tune API is used.** Ollama has none, so specialization is done by generating a `Modelfile` in a temp dir (`fs.mkdtemp(path.join(os.tmpdir(), 'aura-ft-'))`, line 70) and running `ollama create` against it.

- **Invocation style:** `execFile` (not `exec`), so no shell is involved and the model name/path are passed as argv — no quoting/injection risk.
- **Timeout:** 300 s (5 min).
- **Failure handling:** the whole body is wrapped in try/catch; it returns a `FineTuneJob` with `status: 'failed'` and `error: String(e)` rather than throwing (lines 87–98).

The other path in the same file, `fineTuneWithOpenAI()` (line 109), spawns no process — it uploads the JSONL and starts a job via the OpenAI SDK, then polls with `client.fineTuning.jobs.retrieve()` (line 224).

The command is at **`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:74`**:

```ts
await execFileAsync('ollama', ['create', outputModelName, '-f', modelfilePath], {
  timeout: 300_000,
});
```

So the spawned command is:

```
ollama create <outputModelName> -f <tmpDir>/Modelfile
```

**Context on how it's built** (lines 63–76):
- There is no native Ollama fine-tune API, so specialization is done via a generated **Modelfile** written to a temp dir (`fs.mkdtemp(path.join(os.tmpdir(), 'aura-ft-'))`).
- The Modelfile is `FROM <baseModel>` plus a `SYSTEM """..."""` block containing up to 50 `User: … / Assistant: …` snippets pulled from the JSONL training data (lines 43–68). Any `"""` inside the snippets is escaped to `'''` so the heredoc-style block can't be broken.
- It runs through `execFile` (not a shell), so no shell interpolation, with a 300 s timeout. Failures are caught and returned as a `status: 'failed'` `FineTuneJob` rather than thrown.

The other path in the same file, `fineTuneWithOpenAI` (line 109), spawns no process — it uses the OpenAI SDK (`files.create` with `purpose: 'fine-tune'`, then `fineTuning.jobs.create`).

**Answer — the mapping in `checkOpenAIJobStatus`** (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:226-233`):

| OpenAI `remote.status` | Internal `FineTuneJob['status']` |
|---|---|
| `validating_files` | `pending` |
| `queued` | `pending` |
| `running` | `running` |
| `succeeded` | `completed` |
| `failed` | `failed` |
| `cancelled` | `failed` |

The internal lifecycle is the 4-state union declared in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:170`: `'pending' | 'running' | 'completed' | 'failed'`.

Behaviour around the map (lines 235-244):
- **Unknown/unmapped OpenAI status** (e.g. `cancelling`) falls back to `job.status` — the job keeps its previous state rather than being guessed at.
- **Terminal detection** is `status === 'completed' || status === 'failed'`, and only then is `completedAt` stamped.
- `outputModel` is refreshed from `remote.fine_tuned_model`, and `error` from `remote.error?.message` (both falling back to the existing job values).
- Two failure paths bypass the map entirely and force `failed` + `completedAt`: missing `OPENAI_API_KEY` (lines 212-219) and any thrown error from the retrieve call (lines 245-247).

One thing worth flagging: `cancelled` collapsing into `failed` means a user-cancelled job is indistinguishable from a genuine training failure in the stored job record — the only signal is the `error` string, which stays `undefined` for a clean cancellation.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  | OpenAI `remote.status` | Internal `FineTuneJob['status']` |
  |---|---|
  | `validating_files` | `pending` |
  | `queued` | `pending` |
  | `running` | `running` |
  | `succeeded` | `completed` |
  | `failed` | `failed` |
  | `cancelled` | `failed` |
  
  The internal lifecycle is the 4-state union declared in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:170`: `'pending' | 'running' | 'completed' | 'failed'`.
  
  Behaviour around the map (lines 235-244):
  - **Unknown/unmapped OpenAI status** (e.g. `cancelling`) falls back to `job.status` — the job keeps its previous state rather than being guessed at.
  - **Terminal detection** is `status === 'completed' || status === 'failed'`, and only then is `completedAt` stamped.
  - `outputModel` is refreshed from `remote.fine_tuned_model`, and `error` from `remote.error?.message` (both falling back to the existing job values).
  - Two failure paths bypass the map entirely and force `failed` + `completedAt`: missing `OPENAI_API_KEY` (lines 212-219) and any thrown error from the retrieve call (lines 245-247).
  
  One thing worth flagging: `cancelled` collapsing into `failed` means a user-cancelled job is indistinguishable from a genuine training failure in the stored job record — the only signal is the `error` string, which stays `undefined` for a clean cancellation.

**`client.fineTuning.jobs.retrieve(jobId)`** — the OpenAI Node SDK call.

In this codebase it's at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:224`, inside `checkOpenAIJobStatus()`:

```ts
const client = new OpenAI({ apiKey });
const remote = await client.fineTuning.jobs.retrieve(openaiId);
```

Details that matter for how it's used here:

- **ID handling** (line 221): the local job id is prefixed `openai-`, so it's stripped before the call — `job.id.replace(/^openai-/, '')`.
- **Status mapping** (lines 226–235): the remote statuses `validating_files`/`queued` → `pending`, `running` → `running`, `succeeded` → `completed`, `failed`/`cancelled` → `failed`, with the previous local status as fallback for anything unmapped.
- **Fields read off the response**: `remote.status`, `remote.fine_tuned_model` (→ `outputModel`), `remote.error?.message` (→ `error`).
- **Failure handling**: missing `OPENAI_API_KEY` and any thrown error both return a `failed` job rather than throwing — the function never propagates exceptions.

Equivalent REST endpoint, if you need it outside the SDK: `GET https://api.openai.com/v1/fine_tuning/jobs/{job_id}`.

**Answer** — `checkOpenAIJobStatus` (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:210-248`) maps OpenAI's fine-tuning job statuses onto the internal `FineTuneJob['status']` union (`'pending' | 'running' | 'completed' | 'failed'`, defined at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:170`) via the `statusMap` at lines 226-233:

| OpenAI status | Internal state |
|---|---|
| `validating_files` | `pending` |
| `queued` | `pending` |
| `running` | `running` |
| `succeeded` | `completed` |
| `failed` | `failed` |
| `cancelled` | `failed` |

Three details that matter beyond the table:

- **Unknown statuses fall through** (line 235): `statusMap[remote.status] ?? job.status` — any OpenAI status not in the map (e.g. `cancelling`) leaves the job in its previous internal state rather than erroring.
- **Terminal detection** (line 236): `completed` and `failed` are terminal, and only then is `completedAt` stamped (line 242). So a `cancelled` job is recorded as `failed` *and* gets a completion timestamp.
- **Failure paths outside the map**: a missing `OPENAI_API_KEY` (lines 212-219) and any thrown error from `client.fineTuning.jobs.retrieve` (lines 245-247) both force `status: 'failed'` with `completedAt: Date.now()`, bypassing the map entirely.

Note the `openai-` prefix is stripped from `job.id` before the API call (line 221), and `outputModel` is refreshed from `remote.fine_tuned_model` when present (line 241).

  - **Terminal detection** (line 236): `completed` and `failed` are terminal, and only then is `completedAt` stamped (line 242). So a `cancelled` job is recorded as `failed` *and* gets a completion timestamp.
  - **Failure paths outside the map**: a missing `OPENAI_API_KEY` (lines 212-219) and any thrown error from `client.fineTuning.jobs.retrieve` (lines 245-247) both force `status: 'failed'` with `completedAt: Date.now()`, bypassing the map entirely.
  
  Note the `openai-` prefix is stripped from `job.id` before the API call (line 221), and `outputModel` is refreshed from `remote.fine_tuned_model` when present (line 241).

**There is no `isOllamaAvailable` in `alternator.ts`** — that's why Archimedes found nothing. The function is named **`isLocalAvailable`** (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:144`), and it covers both Ollama and LM Studio.

## What it checks (lines 144–160)

```ts
async function isLocalAvailable(baseUrl: string, provider: 'ollama' | 'lmstudio'): Promise<boolean>
```

- Strips a trailing `/v1` or `/` from `archimedesConfig.ollamaBaseUrl`, then GETs **`{root}/v1/models`** — the OpenAI-compatible model-list endpoint.
- Sends `Authorization: Bearer ollama` (or `Bearer lm-studio` when `provider === 'lmstudio'`), because both servers ignore the key but the client requires one.
- Aborts after **`OLLAMA_PING_MS = 3_000`** (line 80) via an `AbortController`.
- Returns `res.ok` — i.e. any 2xx. It does **not** check that the configured `modelName` is actually pulled; a reachable server with a missing model still counts as "available".
- Wrapped in `try/catch` returning `false`: it never throws, so DNS failure, connection refused, or the 3s timeout all collapse to `false`.

## What happens when Ollama is unreachable

Called at line 348, only inside the `decision.useArchimedes && (enabled || forceArchimedes)` branch:

1. **Line 349–350** — `available === false` → `display.warning("Archimedes (Ollama) is not reachable — escalating to large model.")`. The `else` branch (line 351) is skipped entirely, so `archimedesAttempted` stays `false` and no agent loop is ever started for Archimedes.
2. **Line 424** — `usedArchimedes` is still `false`, so the large model runs. Because `archimedesFailureContext` was never set (that's only populated on a real attempt that failed), the large model gets the **plain, un-augmented task** — no "a smaller model already tried this" preamble.
3. **Episode (lines 474–491)** — recorded with `archimedesAttempted: false`, `archimedesSucceeded: false`, `archimedesOutput: undefined`, `tokensUsed.archimedes: undefined`, and `largeModelUsed: <model>`. So the unreachable case is **not** counted as a competence failure against Archimedes — `assessCompetence` sees no attempt, and the gate/score is unaffected.
4. `run()` never throws; the final `result` is the large model's output (or `Large model error: …` if that also fails).

One consequence worth naming: the 3s ping is paid on **every** run where the gate says "use Archimedes", including the epsilon probe, and a `forceArchimedes` (`:small1`) session still silently falls through to the large model rather than erroring — the user only sees the warning line.

**`client.fineTuning.jobs.retrieve(jobId)`**

That's the call — `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:224`, inside `checkOpenAIJobStatus()`:

```ts
const client = new OpenAI({ apiKey });
```

Details that matter in this codebase:

- **Job ID handling** (line 221): the stored ID is prefixed (`openai-ftjob-abc123`), so the prefix is stripped with `job.id.replace(/^openai-/, '')` before hitting the API. Passing the prefixed ID straight through would 404.
- **Status mapping** (lines 226–235): the remote status string is translated to the local `FineTuneJob['status']` union — `validating_files`/`queued` → `pending`, `running` → `running`, `succeeded` → `completed`, `failed`/`cancelled` → `failed`. Unknown values fall back to the job's current status rather than clobbering it.
- **Result fields** (lines 238–244): `remote.fine_tuned_model` fills in `outputModel` once training finishes, `remote.error?.message` carries the failure reason, and `completedAt` is only stamped on a terminal status.
- **Entry point**: callers use `checkJobStatus(job)` (line 174), which routes to this function when the ID starts with `openai-` and to the Ollama tags check otherwise. It never throws — errors come back as a `failed` job.

The sibling call for creating the job is `client.fineTuning.jobs.create({ training_file, model })` at line 139, after `client.files.create({ purpose: 'fine-tune' })` at line 134.

  - **Status mapping** (lines 226–235): the remote status string is translated to the local `FineTuneJob['status']` union — `validating_files`/`queued` → `pending`, `running` → `running`, `succeeded` → `completed`, `failed`/`cancelled` → `failed`. Unknown values fall back to the job's current status rather than clobbering it.
  - **Result fields** (lines 238–244): `remote.fine_tuned_model` fills in `outputModel` once training finishes, `remote.error?.message` carries the failure reason, and `completedAt` is only stamped on a terminal status.
  - **Entry point**: callers use `checkJobStatus(job)` (line 174), which routes to this function when the ID starts with `openai-` and to the Ollama tags check otherwise. It never throws — errors come back as a `failed` job.
  
  The sibling call for creating the job is `client.fineTuning.jobs.create({ training_file, model })` at line 139, after `client.files.create({ purpose: 'fine-tune' })` at line 134.

**`OLLAMA_PING_MS = 3_000`** — declared at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:80`.

**What it's used for:** it's the timeout (3 seconds) on the health-check probe that decides whether a local model server is reachable. It's used in exactly one place in that file — `isLocalAvailable()` at line 144:

- Line 146–147: strips a trailing `/v1` from the configured base URL and builds `{root}/v1/models`.
- Line 148–149: creates an `AbortController` and arms `setTimeout(() => controller.abort(), OLLAMA_PING_MS)` — so the request is killed after 3s.
- Line 150–154: `GET /v1/models` with a bearer token (`ollama` for Ollama, `lm-studio` for LM Studio).
- Line 155–156: clears the timer, returns `res.ok`.
- Line 157–158: any throw (including the abort) is swallowed and returns `false` — the function never throws.

So the constant is the "is the local box up?" budget: 3 seconds to answer, otherwise treat the local model as unavailable and fall back.

Two things worth flagging:

1. **It's duplicated.** The identical constant and an identical `isLocalAvailable()` live in `aura-code-src/src/orchestration/strategy.ts:243` (used at line 250), where it drives default strategy selection from local capability. Two copies of the same 3s timeout that can drift apart — a candidate for a shared constant if you ever want to tune it.
2. **The name is slightly misleading.** It applies to LM Studio as well as Ollama (the `provider` parameter switches the auth header), so it's really "local endpoint ping timeout," not Ollama-specific.

The function that does this job is `isLocalAvailable` at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:144`. (The prior attempt failed because it searched for the wrong name and then gave up.)

## What the check does — `isLocalAvailable(baseUrl, provider)` (lines 140–160)

It's a plain HTTP liveness ping to the local OpenAI-compatible server, not a model check:

- **URL**: takes `archimedesConfig.ollamaBaseUrl`, strips a trailing `/v1` and trailing slash, then appends `/v1/models` — so `http://localhost:11434` → `http://localhost:11434/v1/models`.
- **Method**: `GET` with a dummy bearer token — `ollama` for Ollama, `lm-studio` for LM Studio (LM Studio rejects requests without one).
- **Timeout**: aborts after `OLLAMA_PING_MS = 3_000` (line 80) via `AbortController`.
- **Result**: returns `res.ok` — i.e. any 2xx. It does **not** verify the configured model (`archimedesConfig.modelName`) is actually pulled; a server with zero models still counts as "available".
- **Never throws**: the whole body is wrapped in `try/catch` returning `false`, so a refused connection, DNS failure, or the 3s abort all collapse to `false`.

## What happens when Ollama is unreachable

Call site is `run()` at line 348, inside the `decision.useArchimedes` branch:

1. `available === false` → it prints a warning (line 350): `Archimedes (Ollama) is not reachable — escalating to large model.` (says "LM Studio" instead when `provider === 'lmstudio'`).
2. The `else` branch is skipped entirely, so **`archimedesAttempted` stays `false`** — no agent loop runs, no tokens are spent, and the competence score for that task pattern is *not* updated (the episode records the attempt as not-made).
3. `usedArchimedes` stays `false`, so control falls into the `if (!usedArchimedes)` block at line 424: it prints the `Large model` header and runs the full task on `largeModelProvider` with the session's normal permissions.
4. Because `archimedesFailureContext` was never set (that's only populated on an actual attempt that failed), the large model receives the **bare original task** — no "a smaller model already tried this" preamble. The user just sees the warning and then a normal large-model run.

Net effect: unreachable Ollama is a silent, cheap, non-fatal degradation — one 3-second ping, one warning line, then straight to the large model. `run()` never throws for this case.

**`OLLAMA_PING_MS = 3_000`** — a 3-second (3000 ms) timeout constant, declared at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:80`.

**What it's used for:** it's the abort deadline for the local-model health probe. In `isLocalAvailable()` (`alternator.ts:144-160`), the code builds an `AbortController`, arms `setTimeout(() => controller.abort(), OLLAMA_PING_MS)` at line 149, then does a `GET {baseUrl}/v1/models` against the local OpenAI-compatible endpoint (Ollama or LM Studio, auth header `ollama` / `lm-studio`). If the endpoint doesn't answer within 3 s, the fetch is aborted and the `catch` returns `false` — the function never throws.

**Why it matters:** that boolean decides whether Archimedes (the local model) is considered usable, which in turn drives strategy selection — local reachable + Archimedes enabled → alternation between local and large model; otherwise the large model handles everything.

**Note:** the same constant and an identical `isLocalAvailable()` are duplicated in `aura-code-src/src/orchestration/strategy.ts:243-261`. Both are hardcoded, not configurable — worth hoisting into one shared module if you ever want to tune the timeout.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  So when no custom `permissions` object is passed (i.e., `permissions` is undefined/omitted), the default permission level of the alternator session is **`'normal'`**.
  
  This also aligns with what I found in `/aura-code-src/src/orchestration/strategy.ts:75`, which states that a run with no permission system defaults to `'normal'` — never `'auto'`.
